Does the EHDS Regulation Impact Norway and Norwegian Companies? As Norway is not part of the EU, the implementation of the EHDS Regulation will not automatically apply to Norway. This document contains your essential medical information, and will thereby help doctors to formulate an adequate treatment and avoid some possible risk (for instance in case of allergy).         "text": "The eHDSI Monitoring Framework (KPIs) offers a variety of maps featuring key performance indicators related to the MyHealth@EU monitoring framework. These maps provide detailed insights into the performance and effectiveness of health services across the European Union. The eHealth Digital Service Infrastructure (eHDSI) facilitates the cross-border exchange of health data including patient summaries and e-prescription. Through &#x27;core services&#x27;, the European Commission is providing a common ICT infrastructure and crosscutting services (terminology, interoperability etc.) to EU countries. They can then set up &#x27;generic services&#x27; to connect national eHealth systems through &#x27;National Contact Points for eHealth (NCPeH)&#x27;, with financial assistance from the Connecting Europe Facility Telecom Programme (2015-2020) and from the EU4Health Programme (2021-2027).\n\n\n\nThe eHDSI Monitoring Framework (KPIs) offers a variety of maps featuring key performance indicators related to the MyHealth@EU monitoring framework. These maps provide detailed insights into the performance and effectiveness of health services across the European Union. The eHealth Digital Service Infrastructure (eHDSI) facilitates the cross-border exchange of health data including patient summaries and e-prescription. Through 'core services', the European Commission is providing a common ICT infrastructure and crosscutting services (terminology, interoperability etc.) to EU countries. They can then set up 'generic services' to connect national eHealth systems through 'National Contact Points for eHealth (NCPeH)', with financial assistance from the Connecting Europe Facility Telecom Programme (2015-2020) and from the EU4Health Programme (2021-2027).\n\n\n\nThe eHealth DSI EU countries Expert Group (eHMSEG) is composed of managers responsible for implementing the NCPeH, and nominated by the participating countries.         "text": "Similar to all other Electronic Health Record (EHR) and Personal Health Record (PHR) approaches the chosen Personal Electronic Health Record (PEHR) architecture relies on the patient's consent in order to share documents and medical data with other care delivery organizations, with the additional requirement that the German legislation explicitly demands a patients' opt-in and does not allow opt-out solutions. This creates two issues: firstly the current IHE consent profile does not address this approach properly and secondly none of the employed intra- and inter-institutional information systems, like almost all systems on the market, offers consent management solutions at all. Hence, the objective of our work is to develop and introduce an extensible architecture for creating, managing and querying patient consents in an IHE-based environment.\n\n**Methods:**\n\n\n\nBased on the features offered by the IHE profile Basic Patient Privacy Consent (BPPC) and literature, the functionalities and components to meet the requirements of a centralized opt-in consent management solution compliant with German legislation have been analyzed. Two services have been developed and integrated into the Heidelberg PEHR.\n\n**Results:**\n\nThe standard-based Consent Management Suite consists of two services. The Consent Management Service is able to receive and store consent documents. It can receive queries concerning a dedicated patient consent, process it and return an answer. It represents a centralized policy enforcement point. The Consent Creator Service allows patients to create their consents electronically. Interfaces to a Master Patient Index (MPI) and a provider index allow to dynamically generate XACML-based policies which are stored in a CDA document to be transferred to the first service. \n\nPersonal Health Record (PHR) and Electronic Health Record (EHR) systems are in widespread use in various projects around the world providing different advantages and disadvantages with respect",

        "text": "Prosjekt Internasjonale standarder 07.12.2016 Bakgrunn Prosjektet har gjennomf\u00f8rt vurderinger for f\u00f8lgende standarder og spesifikasjoner: HL7 FHIR HL7 v3 Messaging IHE XDS openehr spesifikasjoner HL7 CDA Detaljer \u00b7 Pasienters elektroniske innsyn i egen journal Tove S\u00f8rensen Styrem\u00f8te TTL Troms\u00f8, 13. mars 2014 Lokalt pilotprosjekt - nasjonal portal Prosjektet skal gi pasienter elektronisk tilgang til sin journal ved Detaljer \u00b7 Overordnet niv\u00e5 - Niv\u00e5 1 Plassering: 1.6.5.9.1.14 Dok.nr: D10888 DIPS - Rekvirents\u00f8k Gjelder for: Hele SiV Hovedomr\u00e5de: Pasientbehandling Dokumenttype: Prosedyre Utskrift er kun gyldig p\u00e5 utskriftsdato Detaljer \u00b7 Kjernejournal Nytt sertifikat for Kjernejournal er bakt inn i versjonen. Ved Helsedirektoratets planlagte tidspunkt for utf\u00f8ring av byttet vil WinMed 2 automatisk g\u00e5 over til \u00e5 bruke nytt sertifikat i Detaljer \u00b7\n\n\n\nBrukertjenester Innsynslogg Applikasjoner med krav til innsynslogg, m\u00e5 tilby en tjeneste for \u00e5 hente ut HL7 FHIR AuditEvent basert p\u00e5 FHIR profilen Norwegian Audit. Profile definerer at applikasjonen m\u00e5 v\u00e6re en actor AuditEvent Repository som betyr at applikasjonen m\u00e5 lagre Audit Event og tilby en tjeneste for uthenting. AuditEvent er definert i en nasjonal profil som enda ikke er godkjent. I p\u00e5vente av dette kan man se p\u00e5 HL7 FHIR ressursen for AuditEvent[1]. In legal terms, the Regulation defines the European Health Data Space as a harmonized framework governing the processing, access, exchange, and secondary use of electronic health data in the Union. Electronic health data is an umbrella concept covering personal and, in specified cases, non-personal information generated in health care ecosystems, including medical imaging, laboratory results, electronic prescriptions and dispensations, discharge summaries, and other records stored in electronic health record systems. \n\nThe term also covers certain data produced by medical devices and wellness applications in clinical workflows, or otherwise designated under the Regulation\u2019s scope. These definitional boundaries matter because they trigger distinct rights, duties, and interoperability obligations across the EHDS\u2019s two functional pillars: primary use for care, and secondary use for non-care \n\nThe official text - Regulation (EU) 2025/327, adopted on 11 February 2025 \u00b7 Deadlines, Milestones - European Health Data Space (EHDS) March 2027: Deadline for the Commission to adopt several key implementing acts, providing detailed rules for the regulation operationalisation.         "text": "# HUNT for better public health\n\n**DOI:** 10.1177/14034948221102309\n\n**Published:** 2022-09-14\n\n**Authors:** Steinar Krokstad, Erik R. Sund, Kirsti Kval\u00f8y, Vegar Rangul, Marit N\u00e6ss\n\n**Affiliations:** 1HUNT Research Centre, Department of Public Health and Nursing, Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences, NTNU, Levanger, Norway; 2Levanger Hospital, Nord-Tr\u00f8ndelag Hospital Trust, Levanger, Norway; 3Faculty of Nursing and Health Sciences, Nord University, Levanger, Norway\n\n**Keywords:** Community surveys, public health, epidemiological methods, cohort studies, Norway\n\n## Abstract\n\n**Aims::**\n\nThe Tr\u00f8ndelag Health Study (HUNT) has collected population data through comprehensive decennial surveys over the last four decades and has so far collected data from 240,000 participants. \n\nThe participants are identified with the unique Norwegian birth number, which enables them to be followed throughout different life stages, from survey to survey, and to endpoint measures in Norwegian national health registers without attrition bias.\n\n**Methods::**\n\nThe study design of HUNT offers several advantages: it provides an overview of the public health development in the population over decades, the data can be used in health services research, clinical epidemiology, studies of causation, trajectories, and consequences of diseases, and to study gene \u00d7 environment interactions.\n\n**Results::**\n\nHUNT data have shown major shifts in public health trends, such as decreasing mean blood pressure and resting heart rate among adults, increasing prevalence of obesity, geographical and socioeconomic inequalities in health, increasing mental health distress among adolescents and young adults with an opposite development among the elderly. \n\nData from HUNT have been used in several major international research projects, where data harmonization with several other population cohorts internationally has been done. HUNT has placed great emphasis on safeguarding research ethics, privacy, and data s",

        "text": "Nord-Tr\u00f8ndelag is fairly representative of Norway except for the lack of large cities and immigrant populations, and the region is suitable for longitudinal studies due to low migration. The HUNT Study data can be linked to a wide range of local and national health registries by means of the unique identification number allocated to all Norwegian residents. All residents \u226520 years of age in Nord-Tr\u00f8ndelag were invited to the HUNT1 (1984-86, 77 202 participants, 89.4% of invitees participated),^[Bibr c1]^ HUNT2 (1995-97, 65 228 participants, 69.5%)^[Bibr c2]^ and HUNT3 (2006-08, 50 800 participants, 54.1%)^[Bibr c3]^ surveys ([Fig fig1]). Since 1995-97, all adolescents (13-19 years of age) in Nord-Tr\u00f8ndelag have been invited to participate in the corresponding Young-HUNT Study.^[Bibr c4]^\n\n## What is the reason for the new data collection?\n\nIn 2017-19, all adult residents of Tr\u00f8ndelag were invited to the HUNT4 survey. \n\nA fourth reason for new data collection is the expansion of genetic and molecular epidemiology, where 70 517 participants of the HUNT2 or HUNT3 surveys have been genotyped.\n\n## What will be the new areas of research?\n\nExtended follow-up of HUNT participants and high attendance among older adults enable a stronger focus on disease incidence, progression and life-course health trajectories. New generations of HUNT participants enable more within-family and inter-generational studies. More valid studies of physical activity and anthropometry are possible due to new data including accelerometer recordings and body composition analyses using bioelectrical impedance. Other new assessments include cognitive and physical testing in old age, fecal sampling for microbiome studies, and metabolomics and proteomics analyses for deeper molecular phenotyping. With available genotype information, analyses are expanded to e.g. genome-wide association studies and Mendelian randomization.",

        "text": "The Tr\u00f8ndelag Health Study (The HUNT Study) is a\n[cohort health study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohort_study) performed in the Norwegian county of [Tr\u00f8ndelag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tr\u00f8ndelag). [[1]](./The_Tr\u00f8ndelag_Health_Study#cite_note-1) HUNT is considered one of the most extensive cohort studies ever conducted in any country. [[2]](./The_Tr\u00f8ndelag_Health_Study#cite_note-2) The HUNT Research Centre, which is responsible for collecting and providing access to the data and samples from the study, is part of the Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences at the [Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_University_of_Science_and_Technology). [[3]](./The_Tr\u00f8ndelag_Health_Study#cite_note-3)\nThe study was primarily set up to address\n\n\nThe aim of the award is to stimulate interest and the work for public health and social conditions that have significance for this. [[7]](./The_Tr\u00f8ndelag_Health_Study#cite_note-7)\nThe HUNT Research Centre is divided into a biological repository (\n[HUNT Biobank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HUNT_Biobank)) and a database made up by more than 5,500 variables (HUNT Databank).\nData from the HUNT Study are available to institutions and researchers who satisfy some basic requirements, whether affiliated in Norway or abroad.\n[[8]](./The_Tr\u00f8ndelag_Health_Study#cite_note-8)\nAny institution with research skills can apply for permission to analyze HUNT data. Each project must be approved by the Regional Committee for Medical Research Ethics ( REK). If data from sources that are not regulated by the Health Research Act are used, the project must also be reported to the Privacy Ombudsman for Research (NSD).",

        "text": "-\n04 May 2021 | Policy Analysis\nCreation of a single portal for health data\n4.1. Physical resources\nIn spring 2020 the Norwegian Government suggested to amend the existing Health Registry Act and other relevant data regulations to establish a single portal for all secondary use of health data with a common system of governance. The reform builds on the work undertaken under the National Health Data Programme, which was established in 2017 by the Directorate for e-Health (NDE), and the Health Registries for Research project (2014-2019), which was aimed at improving access to existing health data for health care improvement, monitoring, management and research purposes (see section 2.6 in the online HiT). \n\nThe portal was established in autumn 2020 and contains information about various data sources, variables, electronic application forms and guidance on the process of requesting access to health data. The National Data Service will be co-located with the National Health Archives at Tynset - a rural location in the Eastern part of Norway.\nThe detailed regulation for National Data Service will be subject to a public hearing in spring 2021. The unit is expected to be operational - with delegated authority to authorize access to health data - from September 2021, when production of datasets may start. A dashboard of process and outcomes indicators have been established to monitor the implementation of the reform. Data about the supplied medicinal product will be sent back to their home country.\n    \u2022 PIN for ePrescription A contains information for patients provided by their home country in their official language(s).\n    \u2022 PIN for ePrescription B contains information for patients provided by the country of travel in several available EU languages.\n\n\n  \u2022 Patient Summaries (PS) provide information on important health aspects such as allergies, current medication, previous illness, surgeries, etc. It is part of a larger collection of health data called an Electronic Health Record. The digital Patient Summary is meant to provide health professionals with essential information in their language about patients travelling from another EU country.\n    \u2022 PIN for Patient Summary A contains information for patients provided by their home country in their official language(s).\u00a0    \u2022 PIN for Patient Summary B contains information for patients provided by the country of travel in several available EU languages.\n\nAdditional information can be found on national health websites.\n\nPatient is a resident from Spain\n\nePrescriptions and Patient Summaries\nEnglish\u00a0 \u00a0 \u00a0 \u00a0 \u00a0 \u00a0\u00a0ePrescription APatient summary A\nSpanishePrescription APatient summary A\n\n\u00a0\n\nPatients travelling to Spain",

        "text": "Skip to main content\nShaping Europe\u2019s digital future\n  \u2022 REPORT / STUDY\n  \u2022 Publication 16 June 2025\n\nDigital Decade 2025: eHealth Indicator Study\n\nThis report presents the eHealth target monitoring results as of 31 December 2024 for the EU-27, Iceland, and Norway under the Digital Decade Policy Programme.\n\nDigital Decade logo followed by text: \"The 2025 Digital Decade eHealth Indicator Study\"\n\nAccess to electronic health records continues to increase in Europe\n\nIn 2024, Member States have made further progress toward achieving the eHealth target of \u201c100% of EU citizens having access to their electronic health records by 2030.\u201d The composite eHealth score for the EU-27 has reached an average of 83%, an increase of 4 percentage points compared to 2023.\n\n\n\nOverall, 16 Member States, Iceland and Norway increased their maturity score compared to 2023, as more data types are available and more healthcare providers are connected and sharing data, among other advancements in access technology and coverage. The advancement is underpinned by substantial increases in composite maturity by Czechia (+26 points), Romania (+17 points), Ireland (+14 points), and Bulgaria (+10 points).         "text": "Federated learning (FL) facilitates collaborative model training across multiple institutions while preserving privacy by avoiding the sharing of raw data, a critical consideration in medical imaging applications.\n\nBackground Federated learning (FL) facilitates collaborative model training across multiple institutions while preserving privacy by avoiding the sharing of raw data, a critical consideration in med...\n\nMed Phys. 2025;52:e18064. https://doi.org/10.1002/mp.18064 ... Federated learning (FL) facilitates collaborative model training across multiple institutions while preserving privacy by avoiding the sharing of raw data, a critical consideration in medical imaging applications. Despite its potential, FL faces challenges such as high-dimensional data, heterogeneity among datasets from different centers, and resource constraints, which limit its efficiency and effectiveness in healthcare settings. \n\nThis study aims to present a novel adaptive FL framework to address the challenges of data heterogeneity and resource constraints in medical imaging. The proposed framework is designed to optimize computational efficiency, enhance training processes, improve model performance, and ensure robustness against non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) data across decentralized data sources.\n\nOur adaptive FL approach efficiently handles large, heterogeneous medical imaging data, reducing training time and computational overhead, while improving model accuracy. The substantial reduction in energy consumption and accelerated convergence make it suitable for real-world healthcare settings. The authors have no conflicts to disclose. The dataset used in this study is publicly available through the Prostate Imaging Cancer AI (PI-CAI) challenge42 and can be accessed online. \n\nThe code for our FL framework is available on GitHub at https://github.com/npiran/federated_learninggithub.com/npiran/federated-learning for reproducibility. 1Shilo S, Rossman H, Segal E.         "text": "The process was carried out within a Baltic-Nordic collaboration from 2023 to 2025, as part of a research project (FederatedHealth: A Nordic Federated Health Data Network) aiming to develop a Nordic federated health data network to enable privacy-preserving analysis of multilingual electronic health records.\n\nFirst, key questions were defined and existing evidence was explored through (i) a survey of domain experts (researchers, data governance specialists and infrastructure providers), (ii) a targeted literature review of FL applications in health research and (iii) systematic mapping of relevant EU-level legislation and policy frameworks. Evidence from these sources was synthesized to identify technical, organizational, legal and sustainability-related requirements for FL-based research. Second, preliminary roadmap components were refined through stakeholder engagement in an online workshop, where feasibility, scalability and sustainability considerations were explicitly discussed. \n\nThird, the roadmap was validated and iteratively refined by an expert panel through a structured group discussion, focusing on long-term sustainability, governance and transferability across research contexts.     "text": "I now have comprehensive coverage. Let me build the response. This is a long, dense technical/research synthesis request \u2014 Tiger's preferences favor token-dense, no-padding output. Given the breadth (Norway EHR, cross-border flow, audit/consent/minimisation, innovation, research papers, EU parallels), this is an extended response. Prose with light headers fits Claude's formatting guidance for technical reference content.\n\n---\n\n# Norway's EHR infrastructure, cross-border flow under MyHealth@EU, and the innovation pipeline\n\n## Part 1 \u2014 How Norway runs its EHR estate\n\nNorway's health system is built on a **layered national infrastructure** rather than a single monolithic EHR. The shared services that everything plugs into are Helsenorge, Kjernejournal, e-resept, HelseID, Norsk Helsenett, and a shared medication-list development effort \u2014 alongside Helseplattformen and DIPS as the actual hospital EHR products.\n\nThe pieces, briefly:\n\n- **Norsk Helsenett (NHN)** \u2014 the closed, secure network all healthcare actors connect through. NHN is the state enterprise responsible for developing and maintaining the national ICT infrastructure for secure communication and sharing of health and personal information among healthcare professionals, including the electronic prescription system. Think of it as the trusted backbone \u2014 no Helsenett certificate, no traffic.\n- **Kjernejournal (Summary Care Record)** \u2014 a digital record providing access to essential health information such as allergies, medications, and vaccinations. This is the national-level \"minimum dataset\" that any treating clinician in Norway can pull.\n- **E-resept** \u2014 the national e-prescription rail. Prescribers, pharmacies and suppliers exchange via standardised messages routed through the central prescription database (Reseptformidleren).\n- **Helsenorge.no** \u2014 the digital gateway for citizens to access medical records, book appointments, and consult with healthcare providers online. Citizen-facing portal, BankID-authenticated.\n- **HelseID** \u2014 the OIDC-based identity broker for healthcare professionals and systems. This is what carries the *who-are-you* and *what-role* claims into every transaction.\n- **Helseplattformen** \u2014 the regional Epic implementation in Central Norway (Helse Midt), one shared EHR across hospitals, GPs and municipalities in that region. Controversial rollout, heavy ongoing implementation research.\n- **DIPS** \u2014 the dominant hospital EHR vendor across the other three regions (Helse S\u00f8r-\u00d8st, Vest, Nord).\n- **Helseanalyseplattformen / Helsedataservice** \u2014 the secondary-use side; covered in Part 4.\n\n**Standards stack.** Norway is migrating from older message-based exchange toward **HL7 FHIR R4** as the national interchange standard. On a national level, helsenorge.no, the Welfare Technology programme and SFM (Central Prescription Module in ePrescription solution) have FHIR interfaces in production. Other examples where FHIR is used include Patient Test Results (Pasientens pr\u00f8vesvar), the Health Data Programme and Helseplattformen. HL7 Norway publishes the national basisprofiler-r4 on GitHub and Simplifier \u2014 these constrain the international FHIR resources for Norwegian use (NIN-based patient identifiers, HPR provider identifiers, Norwegian code systems). SNOMED CT and openEHR are also part of the architectural vocabulary, with terminology governance under Direktoratet for e-helse / Helsedirektoratet.\n\n**The \"new rules\" piece.** The big regulatory shift coming at Norway is **EHDS via the EEA**. As Norway is not part of the EU, the EHDS Regulation will not automatically apply to Norway. However, the EHDS Regulation has been considered EEA relevant, meaning it is likely to be implemented in Norway. The EHDS Regulation will first be implemented in Norway when there is a formal decision by the EEA Council. The Norwegian Health Directorate has also been tasked with analysing the impact of the EHDS Regulation on Norway. Helsedirektoratet has already produced an EHDS gap analysis (April 2025) mapping where Norwegian law and infrastructure diverge from the Regulation.\n\n---\n\n## Part 2 \u2014 What happens when a Norwegian gets treated in Spain (the actual mechanics)\n\nThis is the **cross-border flow** under MyHealth@EU (formerly eHDSI). Spain and Norway being a real example matters because of the long-standing Norwegian elderly-care presence on the Spanish coast.\n\n### The architecture in one diagram-worth of words\n\nEvery participating country runs exactly one **National Contact Point for eHealth (NCPeH)**. According to Article 6 of Directive 2011/24/EU, each member state must designate one or more National Contact Points for cross-border healthcare. For e-health issues, the eHealth Network has decided that there should be only one national NCPeH per country. The NCPeH is the *only* gateway that talks to other countries' NCPeHs.\n\nFor Norway, the Norwegian connection project to MyHealth@EU started in January 2023 with a planned duration of 36 months and production go-live for piloting set for 2025. The work is carried out in close cooperation between Helsedirektoratet and Norsk Helsenett SF. Norway aims to establish PS-B and eP-B services. It participated in PPT testing in March/April 2024 with no reported issues. For go-live piloting in 2025, two municipalities have been identified. So as of now, Norway is in **late-stage piloting**, joining the network in 2025 alongside Austria, Cyprus, Denmark, Hungary, Iceland, Italy, Romania, Sweden and Slovenia.\n\nThe two services in scope at launch are:\n- **eP (ePrescription)** \u2014 your Norwegian prescription gets dispensed at a Spanish pharmacy\n- **PS (Patient Summary)** \u2014 a Spanish doctor pulls your essential medical info from Norway\n\nFuture waves add **OrCD (Original Clinical Documents), Laboratory Results, Medical Imaging, Hospital Discharge Reports** \u2014 these are the priority categories that by March 2031, for primary use, should be operational in all EU Member States: electronic imaging, medical results and discharge reports.\n\n### The actual pull request \u2014 step by step\n\nScenario: You're in M\u00e1laga, walk into a clinic with chest pain, the Spanish GP needs your meds list and history.\n\n1. **Identification.** You present a national ID document. The Spanish HCP enters identifying traits into their EHR's MyHealth@EU portal \u2014 typically combinations of birth date, name, document number. The Spanish system has no idea who you are in the Norwegian system yet.\n\n2. **Country resolution & XCPD.** The Spanish NCPeH (Country B in eHDSI jargon \u2014 the country *of treatment*) determines this is a Norwegian patient and issues a **Cross-Community Patient Discovery (IHE XCPD)** query to the Norwegian NCPeH (Country A \u2014 country *of affiliation*). The query carries the patient identifiers.\n\n3. **Norwegian patient match.** A mechanism is triggered on the PS-A, eP-A CDA Server, which is responsible for searching the exact combination of the given patient's ID and date of birth in the SQL database cluster. If unique match, an internal Norwegian patient ID is returned. If not, the request fails with a defined error code \u2014 no fuzzy matching across borders.\n\n4. **HCP authentication & assertions.** Before any clinical data moves, the Spanish HCP must be authenticated and authorised. The Spanish NCPeH issues two SAML 2.0 assertions: an **HCP Identity Assertion** (who is the doctor, what's their role, which organisation) and a **TRC \u2014 Treatment Relationship Confirmation Assertion** (the doctor has asserted a treatment relationship with this specific patient, with timestamp). These assertions ride with every subsequent request.\n\n5. **Consent check on the Norwegian side.** The Norwegian NCPeH checks whether the patient has a valid cross-border sharing consent on file. This is the **policy enforcement point**. Norway's consent posture for cross-border is opt-in via Helsenorge (you actively enable cross-border sharing \u2014 Norway hasn't gone the broad opt-out route some countries use). If no consent \u2192 request denied, audit logged, nothing returned.\n\n6. **XCA Document Query/Retrieve.** The Spanish NCPeH issues an **IHE XCA (Cross-Community Access)** query, then a retrieve, against the Norwegian NCPeH. OpenNCP provides XCPD, XCA List, XCA queries and PS/eD to HTM transformation services. The Norwegian side returns a Patient Summary as an **HL7 CDA R2 Level 3 document** (structured XML, not free text) following the European Electronic Health Record Exchange Format (EEHRxF) / International Patient Summary (IPS) profile.\n\n7. **Pivot translation.** This is the elegant bit. The PS isn't translated Norwegian\u2192Spanish directly. The Norwegian NCPeH converts the source from Norwegian local representation to a **pivot CDA in English** (using SNOMED CT, ATC for drugs, LOINC for labs, EDQM for dose forms). The Spanish NCPeH then converts pivot\u2192Spanish for display. When you request the patient's ePrescription via your computer, you will receive it in the patient's language and in your language. The ePrescription is automatically translated in your own language by the underlying digital infrastructure. This is why coded data matters so much \u2014 free-text \"kl\u00f8e i halsen\" won't translate; SNOMED `162397003` will.\n\n8. **Display to clinician.** The Spanish HCP sees the PS in Spanish, with the Norwegian original available as a \"friendly copy.\" Treatment proceeds.\n\n9. **For ePrescription specifically:** the Spanish pharmacist queries with the patient's ID, receives the eP, dispenses, and sends back an **eDispensation** message that propagates back to Norway so the Norwegian system knows the medication was collected (and your e-resept is decremented).\n\n### Authorisation, consent and minimisation \u2014 where each is enforced\n\n| Concern | Enforced at | Mechanism |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Authentication** of the HCP | Country B NCPeH | SAML HCP Identity Assertion, X.509 certificates, 2-way TLS between NCPeHs |\n| **Authorisation** (role-based: doctor vs nurse vs pharmacist) | Country B + Country A | Role attribute in HCP assertion, validated against eHDSI role catalogue. eHDSI defines specific IHE permissions required to perform a dispense operation (XDR). |\n| **Consent** | Country A (patient's home country) | Policy decision/enforcement point at Country A NCPeH. Country A is the source of truth for the patient's preferences |\n| **Treatment relationship** | Country B asserts, Country A trusts (but logs) | TRC assertion in SAML |\n| **Purpose-of-use** | Country B asserts | Purpose-of-use code (TREATMENT, EMERGENCY) in assertion. Break-glass / emergency override is a defined code with extra logging |\n| **Data minimisation** | Country A | PS is by definition a *summary* \u2014 IPS scope is bounded (allergies, current meds, problems, immunisations, recent procedures, devices). The full Norwegian EHR is not exposed. ePrescription is one specific dispensable item, not the prescription history |\n| **Transport security** | Both NCPeHs | 2-way TLS, message-level XML Digital Signatures, encryption |\n\n### Audits \u2014 at every node, in a standard format\n\nAudit is the spine of trust here. The standard is **IHE ATNA (Audit Trail and Node Authentication)**. ATNA is the prime security profile in the IHE toolbox. The profile is composed of three things: reasonable access controls (a functional requirement, IHE doesn't further define it), node authentication, and audit logging.\n\nWhat gets logged, on **both** the Norwegian and Spanish sides, for the same transaction:\n\n- **WHO** queried (HCP identity, organisation, role)\n- **WHAT** patient was queried (de-identified reference where regulations require)\n- **WHEN** with sub-second timestamp\n- **WHERE** (source/destination NCPeH endpoints)\n- **WHY** (purpose-of-use code)\n- **OUTCOME** (success, failure, partial)\n- **WHICH** document/event-codes (IHE transaction IDs)\n\nThe general guidance is to log the query event with the query parameters and not the result of the query. The result of a query may be very large and is likely to be of limited value vs. the overhead. So the audit captures the *fact of access*, not the clinical payload.\n\nThe audit standards stack: ASTM E2147 set up the concept of security audit logs for healthcare including accounting of disclosures; IETF RFC 3881 defined the information model; DICOM Audit Log Message made the information model normative and defined vocabulary, transport binding and schema; IHE ATNA defines the grouping with secure transport.\n\nLogs flow to an **Audit Record Repository** at each NCPeH. The Norwegian patient can request an **accounting of disclosures** \u2014 show me everywhere my data went \u2014 through Helsenorge once the audit log feature is exposed cross-border. Applications with audit log requirements must offer a service to retrieve HL7 FHIR AuditEvent based on the Norwegian Audit profile. The application must be an AuditEvent Repository actor, meaning it must store AuditEvent and offer a retrieval service.\n\n---\n\n## Part 3 \u2014 EHDS and what's changing across Europe\n\nThe European Health Data Space Regulation (EU) 2025/327 introduces the first EU-wide legal framework governing both primary and secondary use and exchange of electronic health data across the European Union. The EHDS Regulation came into force on 26 March 2025 and will become applicable in stages, with the general date of application falling on 26 March 2027 and further major milestones following on 26 March 2029, 26 March 2031 and 26 March 2035.\n\nApplication milestones:\n- **March 2025** \u2014 entered into force; transition begins\n- **March 2027** \u2014 implementing acts adopted; templates for data access applications, secure processing environment requirements, dataset descriptions and the data quality and utility label begin applying\n- **March 2029** \u2014 key parts of the EHDS Regulation will enter into application, including, for primary use, the exchange of the first group of priority categories of health data (Patient Summaries, ePrescriptions/eDispensations) in all EU Member States. Rules on secondary use will also start to apply for most data categories.\n- **March 2031** \u2014 for primary use, the exchange of the second group of priority categories (medical images, lab results, and hospital discharge reports) operational. Rules on secondary use start applying for remaining categories (e.g. genomic data).\n- **March 2035** \u2014 third countries can apply to join HealthData@EU\n\nTwo infrastructure pillars under EHDS:\n- **MyHealth@EU** for **primary use** (the cross-border treatment flow described in Part 2)\n- **HealthData@EU** for **secondary use** \u2014 research, innovation, policy. Researchers submit data access applications to national **Health Data Access Bodies (HDABs)**; data is processed in **Secure Processing Environments (SPEs)** \u2014 researchers never download row-level data, they execute code inside the secure environment and only aggregate, anonymised results leave.\n\nBy June 2025, each EU Member State must appoint a National Digital Health Authority to oversee EHDS implementation. By January 2026, all healthcare providers and EHR vendors must certify their systems for interoperability and security compliance. By 2027, full EHDS enforcement across the EU, with penalties for non-compliant organisations. The certification regime for EHR systems is a major industry shift \u2014 vendors like DIPS, Epic-via-Helseplattformen, Cerner etc. will have to demonstrate conformance to the European interoperability and security requirements.\n\nThe AI Act dimension is layered on top. A recent JMIR tutorial maps this: the goal is to show how AI-specific metadata can be included in HL7 CDA and FHIR messages without disrupting standard structures, ensuring both compliance and trustworthiness in AI-assisted clinical decisions. So when an AI system is used in the clinical pathway that produces data flowing through MyHealth@EU, the AI-Act provenance metadata travels with it.\n\n---\n\n## Part 4 \u2014 Norway's secondary-use side: Helsedataservice and Helseanalyseplattformen\n\nSeparate from primary-use (treatment) is **secondary use** \u2014 research, public health, innovation. Norway has been building toward this for a decade.\n\nNorway has a structure with many users of health data for secondary purposes and a broad range of data controllers/data administrators and decision-making authorities that make decisions about access to health data. Many of the data controllers have a long history and manage most of the minimum data categories covered by EHDS. Decision-making authority for 11 statutory health registries was in 2023 transferred to Helsedataservice (HDS), at the Norwegian Institute of Public Health. Through the Health Data Programme, Norway has established an infrastructure that gathers necessary information about health registries in one place, made available via www.helsedata.no.\n\nThe **registry landscape** is Norway's real superpower. National statutory registries (Norwegian Patient Registry NPR, Cancer Registry, Cause of Death Registry, Medical Birth Registry, Prescription Registry/Reseptregisteret, immunisation registry SYSVAK, etc.) cover the whole population and can be linked via the **f\u00f8dselsnummer** (national ID). This is what makes studies like HUNT possible \u2014 the Tr\u00f8ndelag Health Study has collected population data through comprehensive decennial surveys over the last four decades and has so far collected data from 240,000 participants. The participants are identified with the unique Norwegian birth number, which enables them to be followed throughout different life stages, from survey to survey, and to endpoint measures in Norwegian national health registers without attrition bias.\n\n**Important note on the central platform:** It was recently decided to pause work on Helseanalyseplattformen. The big-bang central analytics platform was paused, and Norway is now focusing on simplifying registry access via Helsedataservice and helsedata.no rather than building one super-platform. EHDS will likely force a re-architecture: the EU is introducing the European Health Data Space (EHDS), a new regulatory framework that will make it easier and safer to share health data in Europe, and which sets specific requirements for secure analysis rooms (Secure Processing Environments \u2013 SPE), data governance, and cross-border data sharing.\n\nThe **FAIR SPUHiN project** (Secure Provision and Use of Health Data in Norway) is the current work defining what a \"secure analysis room\" looks like in Norway to align with EHDS Article 73 SPE requirements.\n\n---\n\n## Part 5 \u2014 Innovation and research: what's happening in Norway\n\n### Active national initiatives\n\n- **BigMed** \u2014 a digital platform for Precision Medicine, funded by the Norwegian Research Council and partners to address barriers to clinical implementation of precision medicine and pave the way for big data analytics. BigMed was part of starting up a national network for AI in healthcare with several groups in the healthcare service from all over the country. Officially completed but its outputs feed downstream projects.\n\n- **Norwegian Centre for E-health Research (NSE)** in Troms\u00f8 \u2014 the national research hub. Active workstreams on AI implementation in healthcare, Helseplattformen follow-research, telemedicine, COPD home monitoring. Recent output includes a follow-research study on **BoneView** (an AI fracture-detection decision-support tool) deployed at Vestre Viken 2020\u20132025, presented March 2026 \u2014 finding that the value of AI is not created primarily by the technology alone, but in the interplay between solution, organisation and people. This is implementation science, not algorithm benchmarking \u2014 and it's exactly the angle EHDS regulators care about.\n\n- **KIN \u2014 national network for AI in the health service** \u2014 a national network for artificial intelligence in the health service that consists of various professional environments from all over the country. The network is open to anyone who wants to participate and share their work.\n\n- **HUNT4** \u2014 in 2017\u201319, all adult residents of Tr\u00f8ndelag were invited to the HUNT4 survey. New assessments include cognitive and physical testing in old age, faecal sampling for microbiome studies, and metabolomics and proteomics analyses for deeper molecular phenotyping. With available genotype information, analyses are expanded to e.g. genome-wide association studies and Mendelian randomisation.\n\n- **Helseplattformen implementation research** \u2014 NTNU + SINTEF + NSE following the Epic deployment in Central Norway. This is generating papers on usability, clinician burnout, structured-data quality and adoption that are internationally significant given Epic's footprint elsewhere.\n\n- **Nordic Commons** \u2014 Nordic Council of Ministers project to build a shared Nordic infrastructure for cross-border health data sharing for secondary purposes. This sits parallel to HealthData@EU.\n\n### Cross-European research streams worth tracking\n\n- **xt-EHR Joint Action** \u2014 EU-funded work defining the next-generation interoperability profiles (EEHRxF) that all Member States including Norway are contributing to\n- **TEHDAS II** \u2014 preparatory action for HealthData@EU\n- **POTENTIAL** \u2014 patient-centred cross-border health services\n- **COMPASS-AI** \u2014 newer EU4Health AI-in-cross-border-care project\n- **EU HEROES** \u2014 EHDS preparedness policy dialogue\n- **FederatedHealth** \u2014 a Baltic-Nordic collaboration from 2023 to 2025, part of a research project (FederatedHealth: A Nordic Federated Health Data Network) aiming to develop a Nordic federated health data network to enable privacy-preserving analysis of multilingual electronic health records. Output: a roadmap for federated learning projects using health data, published in Frontiers in Digital Health (2025).\n\n### Recent papers worth pulling\n\n- **Choudhury et al., JMIR AI 2025** \u2014 Personal Health Train / Vantage6 federated learning for tumour segmentation, with secure aggregation server design preventing data leakage. Directly relevant to EHDS SPE architecture.\n- **Federated learning roadmap for EU health data (Frontiers Digital Health, 2025)** \u2014 the Nordic-Baltic synthesis above.\n- **AI Act Compliance Within the MyHealth@EU Framework: Tutorial** (JMIR, late 2025) \u2014 practical mapping of AI Act metadata into HL7 CDA and FHIR.\n- **Digital Decade 2025 eHealth Indicator Study** (EU, June 2025) \u2014 the official scorecard. In 2024, Member States have made further progress toward the eHealth target of \"100% of EU citizens having access to their electronic health records by 2030.\" The composite eHealth score for the EU-27 has reached an average of 83%, an increase of 4 percentage points compared to 2023. Data about identification (99%), personal information (98%), ePrescription (89%) and eDispensation (87%) are most available in a timely manner. Norway is included.\n- **Prescribing beyond borders** (ScienceDirect, late 2024) \u2014 as of August 2024, the CBeP service has expanded its availability to eleven EU member states: Estonia, Finland, Croatia, Portugal, Poland, Spain, Greece, Cyprus, Czech Republic, Lithuania and Latvia. Variations in providing patient counselling due to language barriers highlight the need for improved communication tools. Addressing concerns about drug interaction monitoring and technical rigidity is essential for seamless cross-border adoption.\n\n---\n\n## Quick mental model to anchor everything\n\nThree concentric rings:\n1. **Inside Norway** \u2014 Norsk Helsenett carries traffic, HelseID authenticates, Kjernejournal is the national summary view, DIPS/Helseplattformen are the hospital EHRs, e-resept is the prescription rail, helsenorge.no is the citizen portal. FHIR R4 + SNOMED CT + ATC + LOINC for content.\n2. **Norway \u2194 EU (primary use)** \u2014 Helsedirektoratet + Norsk Helsenett operate the Norwegian NCPeH, which is the *only* gateway to MyHealth@EU. OpenNCP software, IHE XCPD/XCA transactions, SAML HCP+TRC assertions, ATNA audit at both ends, CDA pivot translation through English with coded vocabularies.\n3. **Norway \u2194 EU (secondary use)** \u2014 Helsedataservice as the access-decision body for registries, EHDS-aligned SPEs as the analysis environment, HealthData@EU as the cross-border secondary-use network, Nordic Commons as the Nordic regional layer.\n\nThe current pressure point: Norway is **mid-pilot for MyHealth@EU**, has a **strong registry legacy**, needs to **redesign its secure analysis room story post-Helseanalyseplattformen pause**, and is doing all of this while the EHDS implementing acts are still being drafted through March 2027.\n\nTell me where you want to drill \u2014 the SAML assertion structure, the CDA\u2192FHIR transition Norway is planning, EHDS SPE technical requirements, or where the Norwegian innovation funding is actually landing in 2026.",

INTERVIEW FRAMEWORK: Intro=positioning+through-line+why now. Working style=methodology under non-ideal conditions+real example. Weakness=real gap+self-awareness+mitigation+target. Passion=direction+role anchor. Blank mind=3 types: never done it/brain froze/lost thread. Offer moment=pause before accepting. Panel=one conversation distributed. Never badmouth employers. Never mention salary first. Never vague answers twice.\n7. ANIMA v3.3 EMOTIONAL REALITY: Decode 3 layers before writing. L1=operational failure mode. L2=personal professional cost to hiring manager when it breaks. L3=emotional need beneath the hire (trust/relief/confidence). Letter must land on L2+L3 not just L1. Generic pain = L1 only. Real hooks live in L2.\n8. ANIMA v3.3 RECRUITER READER RULE: Write ONE placement identity sentence \u2014 not a list of domains. Recruiter builds client matching possibilities not Tiger's identity. Formula: \"supply chain and operations profile spanning [domain 1], [domain 2] and [domain 3] systems.\" Single CRM category, multi-context client mapping. Widening scope happens in placement intent line at the end, not in the opening. Never list separate industry pillars \u2014 fragments filing category.\n9. PSYCHOLOGICAL CLUSTER ARCHITECTURE: (1)FEEL UNDERSTOOD: L2 pain + proposed solution. (2)SEE THE SOLUTION: combination as answer to their need + hyper-specific research detail + open loop. (3)BELIEVE THE EVIDENCE: flowing narrative + numbers alone + resistance moment + mirror sentence. (4)TRUST THE PERSON: positive anchor + gaps confident + Norwegian calibrated. (5)KNOW WHAT NEXT: research table + first 90 days as conversation + inevitable close with specific mental image.\n10. COMBINATION SIGNAL RULE: Frame combination as the answer to the reader's specific need, not as Tiger's credentials. Janteloven: \"It is an unusual combination \u2014 [elements]. It came together through specific choices, not by accident.\" MSc + VERA-SNARK + Veoy + 4yr operations + Mechanical Engineering = academic foundation + live research + operational experience + technical literacy. Never rank against the room. Let reader conclude the rarity.\n11. S\u00d8KNADSBREV PRINCIPLE: Trailer not summary. Value first always \u2014 earn the right to name the gap. Force the open with a handoff. The best s\u00f8knadsbrev makes opening the cover letter feel inevitable. No rigid formula \u2014 let the strongest signal lead. Sometimes the unexpected proof leads. Sometimes the failure mode leads. Sometimes the proposed solution leads. Raahul rule: value earned before gap named. Never summarise. Never perform.\n12. ANXIETY KILLER + READING FATIGUE: Kill one named fear per letter \u2014 short paragraph, name the fear, evidence directly, operational consequence. Length follows content not sentence count. Reading fatigue: three fatigue points before sending \u2014 (1) combination cluster too long, (2) more than 2 consecutive gaps without positive anchor, (3) first 90 days as task list not thinking. Fix any that fail before sending.\n13. ANIMA v3.3 JANTELOVEN COMBINATION RULE: Never say \"most candidates\" or rank Tiger against the room. Norwegian mentality reads direct comparison as arrogant. Instead show the combination as a rare natural convergence. Formula: \"It is an unusual combination \u2014 [element 1] + [element 2] + [element 3]. It came together through specific choices, not by accident.\" Let the reader conclude the rarity. Never announce it.\n14. ANIMA v3.3 REPETITION SCAN: Before sending any letter scan for words appearing more than 3 times. Flag and replace with synonyms or restructure. Common offenders: operational, coordination, process, ensure, leverage, utilize. Human writers vary naturally. Repetition is an AI fingerprint. Apply to every letter and s\u00f8knadsbrev before finalising.\n15. ANIMA v3.3 GAP CLUSTER RULE: Never list more than 2 gaps consecutively. Break gap clusters with a positive anchor in the middle \u2014 Mechanical Engineering line, a number, a lesson line. Gap paragraph structure: strength anchor + gap 1 + gap 2 + strength anchor + gap 3 (Norwegian). Cumulative gap listing creates fatigue and momentum loss even with confident framing.\n16. ANIMA v3.3 STRATEGIC HIRE RULE: For company-specific applications research the company's actual strategic direction before writing. Three layers: (1) Map Tiger's profile to where the company is GOING not just what the role requires. (2) Frame research assets as a preview of what Tiger would build FOR THEM. \"This is what I built for X. For [company] the equivalent would be Y.\" (3) Show Tiger as the solution to a future problem they are already naming. Candidate vs strategic hire difference.\n17. ANIMA v3.3 FIRST 90 DAYS RULE: First 90 days must read as a thinking approach not a sequential action list. Show the conversation Tiger would facilitate, the question Tiger would ask, the insight Tiger would surface. Formula: who Tiger talks to first + what each person knows that the other does not + what that conversation produces + what gets built after. Never five bullet-style actions in sequence.\n18. PSYCHOLOGICAL PERSUASION MOVES: Specificity signal=one hyper-specific detail proving real research. Mirror sentence=reflect posting language back in Tiger's own words. Open loop=end cluster 2 with incomplete thought cluster 3 answers. Inevitable close=last sentence creates specific mental image of Tiger in the role. Once they imagine it the call is almost inevitable. These four moves make a letter undeniable without adding length.\n19. ANIMA v3.3 NORWEGIAN PLAIN LANGUAGE: Norwegian managers read English as second language and distrust corporate vocabulary. Every sentence must pass the 3-second test \u2014 felt immediately, no pausing on words. Ban: cross-functional coordination, operational complexity, pre-engagement mapping, constraint-based planning, stakeholder conflict management, leverage, operationalise. Replace with plain spoken equivalents. If you would not say it out loud, do not write it.\n20. ANIMA v3.3 CV VS COVER LETTER RULE: CV states the credential. Cover letter deploys the consequence of the credential applied to the reader's specific world. Never restate what is already on the CV. \"I hold a Bachelor in Mechanical Engineering\" is a CV line. \"I understand what maintenance regimes involve before I put them in a schedule\" is the cover letter line. Consequence over credential. Application over statement. Every time.\n21. ANIMA v3.3 CONVERSATIONAL PRESENCE RULE: Never write \"I am writing to [company] specifically because\" \u2014 hiring managers read this 1000 times a day. Replace with something that sounds like Tiger talking directly to the reader. Ask: what would I actually say if sitting across from them. That answer is the sentence. The letter must feel like a conversation not a performance. Distance kills connection. Presence creates it.\n22. ANIMA v3.3 STARZZ DEPLOYMENT: Starzz covers sport/entertainment supply chain, live event logistics, inbound equipment from DACH manufacturers, S&OP under uncertainty, acquisition stability. Deploy for: media technology, live production, entertainment, sports operations, event logistics, fast-growing international companies. Always scan Starzz for domain overlap before writing \u2014 it is frequently more relevant than BEM KEK for technology and media clients.\n23. ANIMA v3.3 MODERNISATION SIGNAL: Tiger builds tools that reduce manual work \u2014 automated dashboards, Python scripts, workflow triggers, Excel models. Tools the client keeps after he leaves. Deploy early as differentiator. Formula: \"Most coordinators keep processes running. I map where manual work is heaviest and build something that reduces it. Your client keeps those tools after [date]. That is the difference between filling a seat and leaving the operation in better shape.\"\n24. ANIMA v3.3 NORWEGIAN GAP CALIBRATION: Two framings. (1) English working language role: Norwegian is not a gap. Say: \"The role requires strong English. That is where I am.\" (2) Norwegian explicitly required: name it directly and early after value is established. \"My Norwegian is early conversational. For a role where [task] happens in Norwegian that is a real constraint. You should know before presenting my profile.\" Value always earns the right to name the gap.\n25. ANIMA v3.3 EXECUTION VS SYSTEMS BALANCE: Systems thinking signal must never be louder than execution signal for recruiter readers. \"Execution\" is the anchor word. Systems thinking is implied through evidence not declared in language. Ban: \"structuring flows,\" \"mapping constraints,\" \"framework thinking\" as opening language. Use instead: \"execution under complex flows,\" \"identify the constraint, stabilise the flow.\" Operator tone beats consultant tone for placement.\n26. ANIMA v3.3 HUMAN ANCHOR RULE: When a recruiter email or future note becomes too structurally clean it loses emotional plausibility. Add one micro-human anchor inside the proof paragraph: \"This pattern has consistently come from working inside time-sensitive operational constraints across international teams.\" Breaks the over-engineered perception. Grounds the profile in real pressure not methodology. Not a story \u2014 just lived constraint reality in one sentence.\n27. HIMOLDE MSc CORE MODULES: Transportation and Distribution. Vehicle Routing. Discrete Event Simulation. Industrial Organization and Competition Policy. Energy Analytics and Sustainable Technologies. Economics of Energy Value Chains. Offshore Energy Logistics Planning. Sustainable Energy Systems Logistics. Thesis: VERA-SNARK, presented NORS 2025 Kristiansund. Programme: 120 ECTS, 2 years, full-time, English, Molde.\n28. HIMOLDE MSc SEMINARS PART 1: Offshore Upstream Logistics. Power BI. Computational Optimization for Production Planning. Decision Modeling and Metaheuristics. Last Mile Delivery Analytics. Dynamic and Stochastic Programming. Disaster Relief and Supply Chain. Scheduling and Timetabling. RFID and IoT Supply Chain Visibility. Incoterms Cargo Insurance Transport Law. Agentic AI in Logistics (standout \u2014 rare module, deploy for modernisation/AI roles).\n29. HIMOLDE MSc SEMINARS PART 2: Digital Technologies Circular Economy. Energy Transitions. Instead of using a traditional fixed privacy budget allocation, it introduces a reputation-aware adaptive strategy that dynamically adjusts the privacy budget based on the contributors\u2019 historical behavior and data quality, thereby improving aggregation performance under the same privacy constraints. Meanwhile, a fair incentive verification layer is established via smart contracts to quantify and confirm data contributions on-chain, automatically executing reciprocal rewards and mitigating the trust and motivation deficiencies in collaboration. \n\nTo ensure enforceable privacy guarantees, the mechanism integrates lightweight zero-knowledge proof (zk-SNARK) technology to publicly verify off-chain differential privacy computations, proving correctness without revealing private data and achieving auditable privacy protection.\n\n... Furthermore, advanced cryptographic mechanisms, such as Attribute-Based Encryption (ABE) and Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZK-Snarks), enhance security and reliability by enabling secure data validation and access control without revealing sensitive information [Zala et al. 2024 \u00b7 , Anusuya and et al. 2022, Raghav and Bhola 2023. The Robust and Privacy-Preserving Decentralized Deep Federated Learning (RPDFL) scheme also contributes to reliability by ensuring robustness and resilience against system dropouts during training [Tian and et al. 2024]. ... This research introduces a novel blockchain-based EHR system designed to significantly enhance security, scalability, and accessibility compared to existing solutions. Current systems primarily utilize SHA-256 for security and either IPFS or centralized storage, which, while effective, have limitations in providing comprehensive data integrity and security. This multifaceted approach ensures robust encryption, efficient consensus, and high fault tolerance. \n\nThe findings highlight the significant advantages of the blockchain-based EHR system, emphasizing its potential to revolutionize healthcare data management by ensuring secure, reliable, and efficient handling of sensitive medical information.\n\n*Keywords:* Blockchain, Electronic Health Records, Hybrid Security, IPFS, PBFT, Argon2, AES, Multi-Factor Authentication, MetaMask, Ganache, Truffle.\n\n## I. Built on a permissioned Ethereum blockchain, the protocol ensures that medical data validity can be confirmed without ...\n\nThe rise of digital healthcare has intensified concerns over data privacy, particularly in cross-institutional medical data exchanges. This study introduces a blockchain-based protocol leveraging Zero-Knowledge Proofs (ZKP), specifically zk-SNARK, to enable verifiable yet privacy-preserving health data sharing. Built on a permissioned Ethereum blockchain, the protocol ensures that medical data validity can be confirmed without disclosing sensitive content. System implementation involves Python-based zk-circuits, smart contracts in Solidity, and RESTful APIs supporting HL7 FHIR formats for interoperability. \n\nPerformance evaluations show promising results: proof verification times remained under 100 ms, with average proof sizes below 2 KB, even under complex transaction scenarios. Gas consumption analysis indicates a trade-off\u2014ZKP-enabled transactions consumed approximately 93,000 gas units, compared to 52,800 in baseline cases.\n\nOude Roelink, B., El\u2010Hajj, M., & Sarmah, D. However, EHRs still manifest poor interoperability and privacy issues are unresolved. \n\nAn electronic health record (EHR) is the digital version of the patient\u2019s health record which includes highly sensitive private information on history, diagnosis, and treatment. Other stored EHR data typically include appointments, billing and accounts, and laboratory tests. Indiana University, the Mayo Clinic, and Vanderbilt University were among the early academic EHR pioneers. In the 1970s, the federal government developed the country\u2019s largest EHR system, VistA, for Veterans Affairs Health Care. By 1992, hardware had become cheaper and more powerful, and advancing computer technologies advanced the popularity of HER [[15577]]. In 2004, EHR was incorporated into the Health Information Technology for Economic and Clinical Health Act (HITECH), reflecting the perceived need to convert medical records to EHR. New incentives and resources were available from the American Recovery and Reinvestment Act (ARRA or \u201cObamaCare\u201d) beginning in 2009.",

        "text": "In this work, we describe a semi-automated data collection system based on digitally stored informed consent of participants (mothers) and an opt-out option for healthcare providers.\n\n2Department of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science, University of Stavanger, Stavanger, Norway ... Accurate observations at birth and during newborn resuscitation are fundamental for quality improvement initiatives and research. However, manual data collection methods often lack consistency and objectivity, are not scalable, and may raise privacy concerns. The NewbornTime project aims to develop an AI system that generates accurate timelines from birth and newborn resuscitation events by automated video recording and processing, providing a source of objective and consistent data. This work aims to describe the implementation of the data collection system that is necessary to support the project\u2019s purpose. \n\nVideos were recorded using thermal cameras in labor rooms and thermal and visual light cameras in resuscitation rooms. Consent from mothers were obtained before birth, and healthcare providers were given the option to delete videos by opting out.\n\nIf maternal consent is active, the app receives a unique study ID and adds it to the dataset. All collected app data is sent to the Liveborn Web application (Laerdal Medical AS, Stavanger). The personal ID number is deleted immediately after the consent check, independently of the received consent status and is never uploaded to the Liveborn web application. Employees are not defined as study objects but have the option to request deletion of videos in which they have participated, within 48 hours after the respective time of birth. This can be done anonymously through a web interface accessible by scanning a QR code provided in each labor and treatment room or entering the webpage directly. \n\nIn the NewbornTime project, the regional ethical committee required antenatal consent from the pregnant women. Accurate observations at birth and during newborn resuscitation are fundamental for quality improvement initiatives and research. However, manual data collection methods often lack consistency and objectivity, are not scalable, and may raise privacy concerns. The NewbornTime project aims to develop an AI system that generates accurate timelines from birth and newborn resuscitation events by automated video recording and processing, providing a source of objective and consistent data. \n\nThis work aims to describe the implementation of the data collection system that is necessary to support the project\u2019s purpose. Videos were recorded using thermal sensors in labor rooms and thermal and visible light cameras in resuscitation rooms.\n\nAt birth, thermal videos from the labor room (or operation theatre) were automatically recorded and stored locally on a server at SUH. A nurse applied NeoBeat newborn heartrate meter (Laerdal Medical AS, Stavanger) on the baby\u2019s chest and documented ToB and time of cord clamp (ToCC) in the Liveborn Observation app. NeoBeat heartrate data was continuously streamed to the app via Bluetooth Low Energy. If the newborn required treatment, visible light video of the treatment area and thermal video from a sensor monitoring the treatment room were automatically captured and stored locally. Employees could anonymously submit an opt-out request within 48 hours post-birth, which was directly stored in Liveborn cloud.",

Data minimisation, transmission, access, and protection requirements are not optional guidance \u2014 they are mandatory for any actor inside Norsk Helsenett. This is where data minimisation gets technically enforced in Norway, not at the application layer.\n\n### ICD-11 timeline confirmed\n\nNorway is leading translation and testing for **planned 2028\u20132029 introduction**. They can then set up 'generic services' to connect national eHealth systems through 'National Contact Points for eHealth (NCPeH)', with financial assistance from the Connecting Europe Facility Telecom Programme (2015-2020) and from the EU4Health Programme (2021-2027). The eHealth DSI EU countries Expert Group (eHMSEG) is composed of managers responsible for implementing the NCPeH, and nominated by the participating countries. \n\nMembers participate in the deployment and operation of eHealth cross border infrastructure and are responsible for setting up National Contact Points for eHealth.\n\nThe eHealth Digital Service Infrastructure (eHDSI) is an infrastructure ensuring the continuity of care for European citizens while they are travelling abroad in the EU. This gives EU countries the possibility to exchange health data in a secure, efficient and interoperable way.\n\nThe eHealth Operational Management Board (eHOMB) is composed of representatives of internal services of the European Commission and the eHMSEG Chairs. By enabling data portability rights under General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), the European EEHRxF will facilitate safe and trusted sharing of health data, thereby fostering research and innovation in the European Health Data Space (EHDS). At the heart of EHDS lies a fundamental shift: establishing a new set of common European standards for health data sharing across IT systems. One of the most significant enablers of this vision is the European Electronic Health Record exchange Format (EEHRxF). ... EEHRxF builds upon the existing HL7 CDA-based exchange format, but now moves into the more modern HL7 FHIR framework. This transition isn\u2019t merely technological\u2014it\u2019s strategic. EEHRxF introduces requirements for structured and coded health data, allowing not just for data viewing and reading, but also for automated processing and secondary use as well. \n\nBy giving EU citizens the right to share their health data across borders, EHDS facilitates better continuity of care.\n\nOne concrete step toward this alignment is the xShare project, that explores how EEHRxF can be practically implemented within national IT systems. xShare is not just a technical experiment\u2014it represents a forward-looking approach to ensure Europe remains interoperable, efficient, and aligned with common digital health developments. The EHDS and EEHRxF represent more than policy\u2014they signal a new era in digital health. By aligning Danish standards with these European initiatives, we can reduce complexity, enhance data quality, and improve system interoperability. The xShare project plays a pivotal role in bridging this transition, demonstrating how European standards can support and strengthen national health infrastructures.",

        "text": "**Keywords:** Biomarkers, Risk factors, Cancer, Type 2 diabetes, Diabetes, Metabolic syndrome, Obesity, Cardiovascular diseases, Addiction, Anxiety, Depression, Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, Osteoporosis, Biomarkers, Epidemiology\n\n## Abstract\n\nNoncommunicable diseases (NCDs) are a leading cause of premature death globally and have common preventable risk factors. In Norway, the NCDNOR-project aims at establishing new knowledge in the prevention of NCDs by combining information from national registries with data from population-based health studies. In the present study, we aimed to harmonize data on key NCD risk factors from the health studies, describe clustering of risk factors using intersection diagrams and latent class analysis, and identify long-term risk factor trajectories using latent class mixed models. The harmonized study sample consisted of 808,732 individuals (1,197,158 participations). \n\nTwo-thirds were exposed to\u2009\u2265\u20091 NCD risk factor (daily smoking, physical inactivity, obesity, hypertension, hypercholesterolaemia or hypertriglyceridaemia). In individuals exposed to\u2009\u2265\u20092 risk factors (24%), we identified five distinct clusters, all characterized by fewer years of education and lower income compared to individuals exposed to\u2009<\u20092 risk factors. We identified distinct long-term trajectories of smoking intensity, leisure-time physical activity, body mass index, blood pressure, and blood lipids. Individuals in the trajectories tended to differ across sex, education, and body mass index. This provides important insights into the mechanisms by which NCD risk factors can occur and may help the development of interventions aimed at preventing NCDs.\n\n## Introduction",

        "text": "The integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into clinical workflows is advancing even before full compliance with the European Union Cross-Border eHealth Network (MyHealth@EU) framework is achieved. While AI-based clinical decision support systems are automatically classified as high risk under the European Union\u2019s AI Act, cross-border health data exchange must also satisfy MyHealth@EU interoperability requirements. This creates a dual-compliance challenge: vertical safety and ethics controls mandated by the AI Act and horizontal semantic transport requirements enforced through Open National Contact Point (OpenNCP) gateways, many of which are still maturing toward production readiness. This paper provides a practical, phase-oriented tutorial that enables developers and providers to embed AI Act safeguards before approaching MyHealth@EU interoperability tests. \n\nThis shift means many such systems will be categorized as high risk under the European Union\u2019s (EU) AI Act, requiring strict controls over multiple dimensions, including risk management, explainability, and postmarket monitoring [[1]]. At the same time, the EU has built a horizontal compliance and interoperability infrastructure through the European Union Cross-Border eHealth Network (MyHealth@EU), which mandates how structured health data flow between member states, passing through the Open National Contact Point (OpenNCP) software\u2019s security gateways, and preserving semantic integrity [[2]]. The result is a dual-compliance framework: the AI Act regulates the functionality, that is, what intelligent systems do with data (vertical), whereas MyHealth@EU ensures the data itself are transported and interpreted consistently (horizontal). Neglecting either axis arrests deployment and introduces regulatory compliance risk.",

Federated learning (FL) mitigates this by training where data reside and exchanging only model updates, but practical deployments still face two core risks: (1) privacy leakage via gradients or updates (membership inference, gradient inversion) and (2) trust in the aggregator, a single point of failure that can drop, alter, or inject contributions undetected. \n\nWe present zkFL-Health, an architecture that combines FL with zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) and Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs) to deliver privacy-preserving, verifiably correct collaborative training for medical AI. Clients locally train and commit their updates; the aggregator operates within a TEE to compute the global update and produces a succinct ZK proof (via Halo2/Nova) that it used exactly the committed inputs and the correct aggregation rule, without revealing any client update to the host. Verifier nodes validate the proof and record cryptographic commitments on-chain, providing an immutable audit trail and removing the need to trust any single party. We outline system and threat models tailored to healthcare, the zkFL-Health protocol, security/privacy guarantees, and a performance evaluation plan spanning accuracy, privacy risk, latency, and cost. \n\nThis framework enables multi-institutional medical AI with strong confidentiality, integrity, and auditability, key properties for clin",

        "text": "Electronic medical records (EMRs) are extremely important for patients\u2019 treatment, doctors\u2019 diagnoses, and medical technology development. In recent years, the distributed healthcare blockchain system has been researched for solving the information isolated island problem in centralized healthcare service systems. However, there still exists a series of important problems such as the patients\u2019 sensitive information security, cross-institutional data sharing, medical quality, and efficiency. In this paper, we establish a lightweight privacy-preserving mechanism for a healthcare blockchain system. First, we apply an interleaving encoder to encrypt the original EMRs. This can hide the sensitive information of EMRs to protect the patient\u2019s privacy security. Second, a (t,n)-threshold lightweight message sharing scheme is presented. The EMRs are mapped to *n* different short shares, and it can be reconstructed by at least *t* shares. \n\nThe EMR shares rather than the original EMRs are stored in the blockchain nodes. This can guarantee high security for EMR sharing and improve the data reconstruction efficiency. Third, the indexes of the stored EMR shares are employed to generate blocks that are chained together and finally form a blockchain. The authorized data users or institutions can recover an EMR by requesting at least *t* shares of the EMR from the blockchain nodes. In this way, the healthcare blockchain system can not only facilitate the cross-institution sharing process, but also provide proper protections for the EMRs. The security proof and analysis indicate that the proposed scheme can protect the privacy and security of patients\u2019 medical information. The simulation results show that our proposed scheme is more efficient than similar literature in terms of energy consumption and storage space, and the healthcare blockchain system is more stable with the proposed message sharing scheme.\n\n## 1. \n\nIntroduction\n\nElectronic medical records (EMRs) play an im",

Most healthcare organizations use centralized identity management systems (IDMs), which not only limit the interoperability of patient identities between institutions of healthcare, but also create isolation between data islands. \n\nThe more important matter is that centralized IDMs may lead to privacy disclosure. Therefore, we propose Health-zkIDM, a decentralized identity authentication system based on zero-knowledge proof and blockchain technology, which allows patients to identify and verify their identities transparently and safely in different health fields and promotes the interaction between IDM providers and patients. The users in Health-zkIDM are uniquely identified by one ID registered. The zero-knowledge proof technology is deployed on the client, which provides the user with a proof of identity information and automatically verifies the user\u2019s identity after registration. We implemented chaincodes on the Fabric, including the upload of proof of identity information, identification, and verification functions. The experiences show that the performance of the Health-zkIDM system can achieve throughputs higher than 400 TPS in Caliper.\n\n## 1.         "text": "Technologically, KONFIDO solution was based on modern technical paradigms, such as photonic Physical Unclonable Functions (p-PUF),2 blockchain-based auditing,3 homomorphic encryption,4 and the deployment of a Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) system.5 \u00b7 One of the main KONFIDO goals was to align with existing or under development European Union (EU) infrastructure (EHDSI) (Figure 1) and therefore it built its solution upon OpenNCP,6 that is, the reference open-source software implementation of OpenNCP standard developed to support the interoperable communication of the so-called National eHealth Contact Points (eHNCPs). OpenNCP was developed in the context of epSOS (European Partners \u2013 Smart Open Services)7 which has also developed the reference Patient Summary (PS) template used to exchange patient information among EU countries.\n\nBackground:The increase of healthcare digitalization comes along with potential information security risks. \n\nThus, the EU H2020 KONFIDO project aimed to provide a toolkit supporting secure cross-bor...\n\nPC provided input regarding the eIDAS and blockchain part of the KONFIDO toolkit and also contributed in the discussion section. EG provided input regarding the multi-agent simulation model and contributed in the introduction and the discussion part. LR supervised the paper as a whole. Our analyses identify the kinds of concerns expressed by stakeholders about electronic data sharing and focus on the factors that ultimately facilitated acceptability of the new exchanges.\n\n****Methods**:**\n\nWe conducted 549 surveys with patients and 66 semi-structured interviews with providers and other stakeholders prior to implementation of the HIEs to assess concerns about confidentiality in the electronic sharing of patient data. The patient quantitative data were analyzed using SAS 9.2 to yield sample descriptive statistics. \n\nThe analysis of the qualitative interviews with providers and other stakeholders followed an open-coding process, and convergent and divergent perspectives emerging from those data were examined within and across the HIEs.\n\n****Results**:**\n\nWe found widespread acceptabilit",

        "text": "Our findings indicate that patient\u2019s attitude toward HIE is affected by seven main factors: perceived benefits, perceived concerns, patient characteristics, patient participation level in HIE, type of health information, identity of recipients, and patient preferences regarding consent and features.\n\n**Conclusions:**\n\nThe findings provide useful theoretical implications for research by developing a classification of significant factors and a framework based on the lessons learned from the literature to help guide HIE efforts. Our results also have fundamental practical implications for policy makers, current and potential organizers of HIEs by highlighting the role of patients in the widespread implementation of HIE. The study indicates that new approaches should be applied to completely underline HIE benefits for patients and also address their concerns.\n\n**Electronic supplementary material:**",
